# Annotated checklist for stony corals of American Sāmoa with reference to mesophotic depth records

Anthony D. Montgomery<sup>1,2</sup>, Douglas Fenner<sup>3</sup>, Robert J. Toonen<sup>1</sup>

--- SENTENCE 2 ---
I Hawaiʻi Institute of Marine Biology, University of Hawaiʻi at Mānoa, Kāneʻohe, HI 96744, USA **2** U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service, Pacific Islands Fish and Wildlife Office, 300 Ala Moana Blvd.

--- SENTENCE 3 ---
Honolulu, HI 96850, USA **3** Ocean Associates, Inc., NOAA Fisheries Service, Pacific Islands Regional Office, Pago Pago, AS, USA

Corresponding author: Anthony D. Montgomery (amont@hawaii.edu)

Academic editor:

--- SENTENCE 4 ---
B. W. Hoeksema | Received 22 March 2019 | Accepted 20 April 2019 | Published 21 May 2019

**Citation:** Montgomery AD, Fenner D, Toonen RJ (2019)

--- SENTENCE 5 ---
Annotated checklist for stony corals of American Samoa with reference to mesophotic depth records.

--- SENTENCE 6 ---
https://doi.org/10.3897/zookeys.849.34763

#### **Abstract**

An annotated checklist of the stony corals (Scleractinia, Milleporidae, Stylasteridae, and Helioporidae) of American Sāmoa is presented.

--- SENTENCE 7 ---
A total of 377 valid species has been reported from American Sāmoa with 342 species considered either present (251) or possibly present (91).

--- SENTENCE 8 ---
Of these 342 species, 66 have a recorded geographical range extension and 90 have been reported from mesophotic depths (30–150 m).

--- SENTENCE 9 ---
Additionally, four new species records (*Acanthastrea subechinata* Veron, 2000, *Favites paraflexuosus* Veron, 2000, *Echinophyllia echinoporoides* Veron & Pichon, 1980, *Turbinaria irregularis* Bernard, 1896) are presented.

--- SENTENCE 10 ---
Coral species of concern include species listed under the US Endangered Species Act (ESA) and the International Union for Conservation of Nature's (IUCN)

--- SENTENCE 11 ---
Red List of threatened species.

--- SENTENCE 12 ---
Approximately 17.5% of the species present or possibly present are categorized as threatened by IUCN compared to 27% of the species globally.

--- SENTENCE 13 ---
American Sāmoa has seven ESA-listed or ESA candidate species, including *Acropora globiceps* (Dana, 1846), *Acropora jacquelineae* Wallace, 1994, *Acropora retusa* (Dana, 1846), *Acropora speciosa* (Quelch, 1886), *Fimbriaphyllia paradivisa* (Veron, 1990), *Isopora crateriformis* (Gardiner, 1898), and *Pocillopora meandrina* Dana, 1846.

--- SENTENCE 14 ---
There are two additional species possibly present, i.e., *Pavona diffluens* (Lamarck, 1816) and *Porites napopora* Veron, 2000.

#### **Keywords**

Helioporidae, mesophotic coral ecosystems, Milleporidae, new records, Scleractinia, Stylasteridae, WoRMS, World List of Scleractinia

#### **Introduction**

American Sāmoa is an unincorporated territory of the United States and lies between Hawaiʻi and New Zealand in the Southern Pacific Ocean (Figure 1).

--- SENTENCE 39 ---
The Samoan Archipelago includes American Sāmoa, which consists of five high islands (Tutuila, Aunuʻu, Ofu, Olosega, and Taʻū), one low island (Swains Island), and an atoll (Rose), and the Independent state of Sāmoa at the west end of the archipelago with the two high islands (Upola and Savaiʻi) and eight smaller islands.

--- SENTENCE 40 ---
Tonga lies approximately 900 km to the southwest and Tuvalu lies approximately 1,400 km to the northwest.

--- SENTENCE 41 ---
Coral reef research has been conducted in American Sāmoa since the early 1900s with work analyzing the growth rate of coral reefs and reporting the World's second stony coral transect (Mayor 1924a, 1924b; Mayor 1918).

--- SENTENCE 42 ---
Since then, there has been a series of studies around the territory documenting its coral communities (Hoffmeister 1925; Birkeland et al. 1987, 2003, 2013; Maragos et al. 1994) including coral species checklists (Lamberts 1983; Birkeland 2001, 2007a, 2007b; Coles et al. 2003; DiDonato et al. 2006; Lovell and McLardy 2008; Kenyon et al. 2011).

--- SENTENCE 43 ---
While there have been several papers looking at the coral species of American Sāmoa, most are not peerreviewed and none to date have considered the mesophotic zone explicitly.

--- SENTENCE 44 ---
This has led to a large amount of documentation on the coral diversity across the territory, but not in a comprehensive manner that analyzes all available data and the scale of evidence for a complete coral species presence.

--- SENTENCE 45 ---
Previously referred to as deep coral reefs or the coral reef twilight zone (Pyle 1996, 1998, 2000), mesophotic coral ecosystems (MCE) are well defined in the literature: "Mesophotic coral ecosystems (MCEs) are characterized by the presence of light-dependent corals and associated communities that are typically found at depths ranging from 30 to 40 m and extending to over 150 m in tropical and subtropical regions.

--- SENTENCE 46 ---
The dominant communities providing structural habitat in the mesophotic zone can be comprised of coral, sponge, and algal species" (Hinderstein et al. 2010).

--- SENTENCE 47 ---
Previously thought to be marginal habitats, MCEs have been hypothesized as potential refugia for shallow water corals under the 'deep reef refugia' hypothesis (DRRH) (Glynn 1996; Hughes and Tanner 2000; Riegl and Piller 2003; Bak et al. 2005; Kahng et al. 2010; Bongaerts et al. 2010; Tenggardjaja et al. 2014; Holstein et al. 2015).

--- SENTENCE 48 ---
Others have argued that MCEs host different communities of species, and make unlikely refugia from a warming ocean (Hurley et al. 2016; Smith et al. 2016; Bongaerts et al. 2017; Semmler et al. 2017).

--- SENTENCE 49 ---
Bongaerts et al. (2010) reviewed the current literature regarding the DRRH for Caribbean reefs and concluded that the DRRH is more likely to apply to "depth generalist" species and may serve a greater importance in the upper range of MCEs (30–60 m).

--- SENTENCE 50 ---
This was later exemplified on a Pacific reef in Okinawa, where the coral *Seriatopora hystrix*

--- SENTENCE 51 ---
Dana, 1846 was extirpated from shallow water, and later discovered in an upper MCE at depths of 35 to 47 m (Sinniger et al. 2013).

--- SENTENCE 52 ---
Here, we provide a notation for each species reported to be within the mesophotic zone in order to provide a common baseline on species occurrence.

--- SENTENCE 53 ---
The biogeography of corals has only been studied in the last few decades (Stehli and Wells 1971) with species level comparisons in the last two decades.

--- SENTENCE 54 ---
Corals of the World

!

** Map of American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 56 ---
A map of American Sāmoa showing its proximity to Independent Sāmoa and the distances between all the island groups (in green) and shallow (< 150 meter depth) banks (in purple) within the territory.

--- SENTENCE 57 ---
(CoTW; Veron et al. (2019) recognizes 833 valid zooxanthellate scleractinian species globally and does not include the azooxanthellate dendrophylliid corals (Cairns 2001;

--- SENTENCE 58 ---
Arrigoni et al. 2014) as well as the cave dwelling *Leptoseris troglodyta* Hoeksema, 2012 (Hoeksema 2012a).

--- SENTENCE 59 ---
The World List of Scleractinia (WLS) reports 1,610 valid species with approximately half of those being zooxanthellate, hermatypic species (Hoeksema and Cairns 2019).

--- SENTENCE 60 ---

Broad-scale biogeographic studies require regional-scale data that can be traced back to a consistent taxonomy (Veron 1995).

--- SENTENCE 70 ---
The most comprehensive biogeographic analysis of scleractinians has been completed by Veron et al. (2015) and CoTW (Veron 2000; Veron et al. 2019).

--- SENTENCE 71 ---
Together, these studies show a pattern of highest diversity in the Coral Triangle with decreasing diversity towards the north, east, and south (Hoeksema 2007; Veron et al. 2015).

--- SENTENCE 72 ---
The ecoregion described by Veron et al. (2019) that includes American Sāmoa is the Sāmoa, Tuvalu, and Tonga ecoregion and includes the island groups of Tuvalu, Tokelau, Wallis and Futuna, Tonga, Niuē, and the Sāmoa Archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 73 ---
Veron et al. (2019) reports this ecoregion has 313 reef coral species, while the neighboring ecoregions range from 16 to over 500 coral reef species and the Coral Triangle with 627 coral reef species (Table 1;

--- SENTENCE 74 ---
Veron et al. 2019).

--- SENTENCE 75 ---
In order to make available the vast history of work completed in American Sāmoa, we present a detailed annotated analysis for the reported shallow and mesophotic stony coral species including scleractinian, milleporid, stylasterid, and helioporid species.

--- SENTENCE 76 ---
This analysis presents the information in an open, transparent manner that allows the reader to judge any particular observation over and beyond our analysis.

--- SENTENCE 77 ---
The goal of this study is to provide a foundation for a thorough species list for the Territory of American Sāmoa with a mechanism that allows the reader to trace back to the original recording of the species.

--- SENTENCE 78 ---
This mechanism will allow different interpretations of the taxonomy, confidence of a species observation, or future analyses of species presence to be re-analyzed or questioned easily.

--- SENTENCE 79 ---
Further, we believe this type of approach to a species checklist on a small regional scale can provide a valuable contribution to broader scale biogeographic analyses as discussed by Veron (1995).

#### **Materials and methods**

Species occurrences in the study area were recorded from all available literature (Mayor 1924a, 1924b; Hoffmeister 1925; Dahl and Lamberts 1977; USACE 1980; Dahl 1981;

--- SENTENCE 80 ---
Birkeland et al. 1987, 2003, 2013; Itano and Buckley 1988; Hoeksema 1989; Hunter et al. 1993; Maragos et al. 1994, 1995; Mundy 1996; Green et al. 1997; Green and Hunter 1998; Green et al. 1999; Mundy and Green 1999; Birkeland and Belliveau 2000; Craig et al. 2001; Fisk and Birkeland 2002; Work and Rameyer 2002; Coles et al. 2003; Wolstenholme et al. 2003; Cornish and DiDonato 2004; DiDonato et al. 2006; Birkeland 2007a, 2007b; Fenner et al. 2008; Lovell and McLardy 2008; Forsman and Birkeland 2009; Forsman et al. 2009; Bare et al. 2010; Benzoni et al. 2010; Kenyon et al. 2010, 2011; CRED 2011;

--- SENTENCE 81 ---
Corals NPAS 2016; Fenner and Sudek 2016; AM 2018; BPBM 2018; DMWR 2018; Fenner 2018; QM 2018; NMNH 2018; Creuwels 2019; Gross 2019; Montgomery et al. 2019; Paulay and Brown 2019).

--- SENTENCE 82 ---
Each appearance of a species name was recorded with as much information as available including the exact spelling of the species name, identification qualifiers (e.g., cf., aff., ?, etc.), survey type, island, species location (e.g., site name, transect label, etc.), the reference of the appearance, if the species was referenced to a different source and its reference, and any other general notes or information.

--- SENTENCE 83 ---
This information was collected in a Microsoft Excel spreadsheet and imported into R where the data were validated.

--- SENTENCE 84 ---
All unique species names were cross-referenced with valid accepted names in the World Register of Marine Species (WoRMS) through their REST webservice [\(http://www.marinespecies.org/rest/](http://www.marinespecies.org/rest/)).

--- SENTENCE 85 ---
Names were queried for exact matches and missing matches were queried for fuzzy matches based on the Taxamatch algorithm (Rees 2014).

--- SENTENCE 86 ---
Remaining names were queried again with older generic names (e.g., *Madrepora* instead of *Acropora*).

--- SENTENCE 87 ---
All remaining names were retained as used.

--- SENTENCE 88 ---
Additional data validation steps included the elimination of duplicate returns from WoRMS, examining each individual fuzzy match return and dubious names, and standardizing nomenclature for unaccepted name explanations.

--- SENTENCE 89 ---
The classification was updated based on the accepted valid name of a species and added to all records from the WoRMS database.

--- SENTENCE 90 ---
Finally, any missing names that were unable to be matched with WoRMS records were added individually.

--- SENTENCE 91 ---
The checklist is arranged by species presence determination and then by order and alphabetically by family, genus and species for each valid name.

--- SENTENCE 92 ---
Each species record starts with listing the valid species name as accepted in WoRMS (WoRMS Editorial Board 2018).

--- SENTENCE 93 ---
The valid name is hyperlinked to the WoRMS species taxa webpage followed by the species authority and AphiaID.

--- SENTENCE 94 ---
Under each valid name, species names as appeared in the literature are reported followed by the species authority and AphiaID.

--- SENTENCE 95 ---
Species names that have grammatical misspellings are labeled with [sic].

--- SENTENCE 96 ---
If the species was a synonym, then it was labeled as a heterotypic or homotypic synonym.

--- SENTENCE 97 ---
We use the term 'homotypic synonym' to refer to cases where the same species epithet is combined with different genera, and 'heterotypic synonym' in cases where different species epithets are regarded as subjective junior synonyms.

--- SENTENCE 98 ---
While the terms 'objective synonym' and 'subjective synonym' are technically defined within the International Code of Zoological Nomenclature (ICZN) Code to mean essentially the same ([http://www.nhm.ac.uk/hosted-sites/iczn/code/index.jsp?booksection=glos](http://www.nhm.ac.uk/hosted-sites/iczn/code/index.jsp?booksection=glossary&nfv=true)

--- SENTENCE 99 ---
[sary&nfv=true](http://www.nhm.ac.uk/hosted-sites/iczn/code/index.jsp?booksection=glossary&nfv=true)), in our experience the term 'objective synonym' is most commonly used in cases of different species epithets that share the same type specimen (e.g., replacement names).

--- SENTENCE 100 ---
Therefore, we believe the terms 'homotypic synonym' and 'heterotypic synonym' are more appropriate in the present context, which is consistent with how these terms are most commonly used for taxonomic purposes.

--- SENTENCE 101 ---
Each species name is followed by the references that used that exact name and spelling.

--- SENTENCE 102 ---
The references are separated into first-hand accounts labeled as reported and second or more hand accounts labeled as referenced.

--- SENTENCE 103 ---
All names (valid and synonymies) according to the World List of Scleractinia (Hoeksema and Cairns 2019) were cross-referenced with the accepted names by Veron et al. (2019), Wallace (1999), and Wallace et al. (2012) to provide the reader with a potentially different view of the species and highlight any differences.

--- SENTENCE 104 ---
Any name that matched a name accepted by Wallace (1999) or Wallace et al. (2012) was noted by CCW.

--- SENTENCE 105 ---
Any name that matched a name accepted by Veron et al. (2019) was notated as CoTW.

--- SENTENCE 106 ---
The notation also provides a hyperlink to the factsheet available on the CoTW webpage [\(http://www.coralsoftheworld.org/page/home/](http://www.coralsoftheworld.org/page/home/)).

--- SENTENCE 107 ---
Veron et al. (2019) is an electronic source that has evolved from the printed worldwide overview of reefdwelling Scleractinia by Veron (2000), while Hoeksema and Cairns (2019) is based on published taxonomic revisions of various scleractinian families and genera, partly based on molecular analyses and/or on the re-examination of type specimens and other museum material (Wallace 1999;

--- SENTENCE 108 ---
Wallace et al. 2007, 2012; Hoeksema 2009, 2012a, 2012b, 2014; Benzoni et al. 2010, 2011, 2012a, 2014; Gittenberger et al. 2011; Huang et al. 2011, 2014a, 2014b, 2016; Budd et al. 2012; Arrigoni et al. 2014, 2015, 2016a, 2016b, 2017, 2018a; Kitano et al. 2014; Schmidt-Roach et al. 2014; Terraneo et al. 2016, 2017).

--- SENTENCE 109 ---
After all names and references are listed, we include our determination of the species presence in American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 110 ---
This determination was split into five categories: present, possibly present, uncertain, not likely present, and not present.

--- SENTENCE 111 ---
This determination was made largely on the type of evidence available including the amount of references, the type of reference, the evidence the reference includes (e.g., in situ observation, photographic, sample identified, or type specimen), and taxonomic and identification certainty.

--- SENTENCE 112 ---
The species presence determination is followed by information on the highest level of evidence available to support the species presence.

--- SENTENCE 113 ---
Additionally, the annotation includes the reported species distribution within American Sāmoa as reported by the literature, the nearest confirmed ecoregion for the species presence according to Veron et al. (2019), the direction of a potential geographic range extension, the evidence of species vulnerability as documented by the International Union for Conservation of Nature's (IUCN)

--- SENTENCE 114 ---
Red List of threatened species (IUCN 2018) as assessed by Carpenter et al. (2008) and the US Endangered Species Act (ESA), and the depths and associated references for corals reported from mesophotic depths.

--- SENTENCE 115 ---
Finally, we provide notes that discuss our justification of a species presence determination, other evidence not already listed, or other noteworthy comments.

--- SENTENCE 116 ---
Each IUCN note is hyperlinked to the IUCN Red List species information webpage ([http://www.iucnredlist.org/\)](http://www.iucnredlist.org/).

--- SENTENCE 117 ---
Species listed by the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) under the ESA are notated with the symbol for threatened and for candidate listing.

--- SENTENCE 118 ---
The species status as listed by the IUCN Red List of Threatened Species is noted as Critically Endangered (CE), Endangered (EN), Vulnerable (VU), Near Threatened (NT), Least Concern (LC), Data Deficient (DD), and Not Evaluated (NE).

--- SENTENCE 322 ---
Veron & Wallace, 1984 (288198).

--- SENTENCE 323 ---
CoTW CCW Reported – Craig et al. 2001; DiDonato et al. 2006; Birkeland 2007a; Corals NPAS 2016.

--- SENTENCE 324 ---
Referenced – DiDonato et al. 2006; Lovell and McLardy 2008; Kenyon et al. 2011.

--- SENTENCE 325 ---
**American Sāmoa status** – Present.

--- SENTENCE 326 ---
**Evidence** – Single specimen report (identified by A Montgomery and D Fenner).

--- SENTENCE 327 ---
**Distribution** – American Sāmoa, Ofu, Taʻū, Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 328 ---
**Nearest confirmed ecoregion** – Sāmoa, Tuvalu, and TongaSāmoa, Tuvalu, and Tonga.

--- SENTENCE 329 ---
**Mesophotic record** – 41 m depth (Montgomery et al. 2019).

--- SENTENCE 330 ---
**Notes** – *Acropora akajimensis* is a synonym of *A. donei*, but Veron et al. (2019) recognize *A. akajimensis* as a valid species while Wallace (1999) and Wallace et al.

--- SENTENCE 331 ---
The *A. akajimensis* reported in Montgomery et al. (2019) was based on a skeletal analysis of a sample that matched the description of *A. akajimensis* very closely, and not *A. donei*.

--- SENTENCE 332 ---
*A. donei* is neat and tidy with the radial corallites and branches being relatively blunt and relatively uniform in thickness.

--- SENTENCE 333 ---
*A. akajimensis* appears much more jagged and disorganized, with pointy corallites and branches.

--- SENTENCE 334 ---
More taxonomic research is needed for these species.

--- SENTENCE 335 ---
The nearest confirmed ecoregion for *A. akajimensis* is New Caledonia (Veron et al. 2019).

--- SENTENCE 336 ---
#### *Acropora eurystoma* **(Klunzinger, 1879) (207108)** CoTW CCW

*Acropora eurystoma* (Klunzinger, 1879) (207108).

--- SENTENCE 337 ---
CoTW CCW Reported – NMNH 2018.

--- SENTENCE 338 ---
Reported – Birkeland et al. 1987.

--- SENTENCE 339 ---
*Acropora pagoensis* Hoffmeister, 1925 (411144) heterotypic synonym.

--- SENTENCE 340 ---
Reported – Hoffmeister 1925; USACE 1980; Lamberts 1983;

--- SENTENCE 341 ---
Birkeland et al. 1987, 2003; Fenner et al. 2008; DMWR 2018; Fenner 2018; NMNH 2018.

--- SENTENCE 342 ---
Referenced – Green et al. 1999; Coles et al. 2003; Birkeland 2007b.

--- SENTENCE 343 ---
**American Sāmoa status** – Present.

--- SENTENCE 344 ---
**Evidence** – Type (synonym *Acropora pagoensis*).

--- SENTENCE 345 ---
**Distribution** – American Sāmoa, Aunuʻu, Ofu/Olosega, Taʻū, Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 346 ---
**Nearest confirmed ecoregion** – Red Sea north-central.

--- SENTENCE 347 ---
**Geographical range extension** – Southeast.

--- SENTENCE 348 ---
In Hoffmeisterʻs (1925) original description of *A. pagoensis*, he cites it as similar to *A. eurystoma*.

--- SENTENCE 349 ---
However, Wallace (1999) and Veron et al. (2019) consider this species a probable synonym of *Acropora tenuis* (Dana, 1846).

--- SENTENCE 350 ---
All citations have used the name *A. pagoensis* except for NMNH (2018) in which one specimen from American Sāmoa was identified by S Cairns as *A. eurystoma*.

--- SENTENCE 351 ---
Hoffmeister (1925) further states this species is distinctive from *A. eurystoma*.

--- SENTENCE 352 ---
We consider *A. eurystoma* present based on *A. pagoensis* being synonymized under *A. eurystoma*, however, we believe *A. pagoensis* may be a valid species and *A. eurystoma* is not likely a valid name for the American Sāmoa observations.

--- SENTENCE 353 ---
Veron et al. (2019) and Wallace (1999) consider this species to be endemic to the Red Sea.

--- SENTENCE 354 ---
We believe more taxonomic investigation into this species is warranted, which should include colonies collected from American Sāmoa.

**Distribution** – American Sāmoa, Aunuʻu, Manuʻa Islands, Ofu, Ofu/Olosega, Rose Atoll, Swains, Taʻū, Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 375 ---
**Nearest confirmed ecoregion** – Sāmoa, Tuvalu, and Tonga.

--- SENTENCE 376 ---
**Vulnerability** – , VU.

--- SENTENCE 377 ---
**Notes** – Fenner (this study) has examined the type specimens of *Acropora humilis* (Dana, 1846) and *A. globiceps*.

--- SENTENCE 378 ---
All colonies examined (including the ones within the DMWR collection) belong clearly to *A. globiceps*.

--- SENTENCE 379 ---
The name *A. globiceps* was forgotten until Wallace (1999) and Veron (2000) used it again.

--- SENTENCE 380 ---
It appears likely that all reports of *A. humilis* from the Samoan Archipelago are actually *A. globiceps*.

--- SENTENCE 399 ---

--- SENTENCE 402 ---
*Acropora surculosa* (Dana, 1846)

--- SENTENCE 403 ---
Reported – USACE 1980; Lamberts 1983; Birkeland et al. 1987, 2003; Maragos et al. 1994; Corals NPAS 2016; Fenner 2018.

--- SENTENCE 404 ---
Referenced – DiDonato et al. 2006; Birkeland 2007b.

--- SENTENCE 405 ---
(Dana, 1846) (207085) heterotypic synonym.

--- SENTENCE 406 ---
Reported – Coles et al. 2003; DMWR 2018.

--- SENTENCE 407 ---
**American Sāmoa status** – Present.

--- SENTENCE 408 ---
**Evidence** – Multiple specimen reports.

--- SENTENCE 409 ---
**Distribution** – American Sāmoa, Aunuʻu, Manuʻa Islands, Ofu, Ofu/Olosega, Olosega, Rose Atoll, Sāmoa Islands, Taʻū, Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 410 ---
**Nearest confirmed ecoregion** – Sāmoa, Tuvalu, and Tonga.

--- SENTENCE 411 ---
While Wallace (1999) and Veron et al. (2019) concur that *Acropora surculosa* (Dana, 1846) is a synonym of *A. hyacinthus*, Randall and Myers (1983)

--- SENTENCE 412 ---
considered *A. surculosa* to be valid.

--- SENTENCE 413 ---
Randall (1995) reports both *A. surculosa* and *A. hyacinthus* from Palau.

--- SENTENCE 414 ---
Colonies in American Sāmoa fit the type of *A. surculosa* (examined by D Fenner) and appears to be different from *A. hyacinthus*.

--- SENTENCE 415 ---
*Acropora surculosa* has smaller colonies with longer cone-shaped branchlets that more often appear to be fused or with multiple branch tips with long tentacles out during the day, while *A. hyacinthus* has larger colonies with shorter cylindrical branchlets that donʻt fuse or have multiple branch tips with much smaller tentacles if exposed during the day.

--- SENTENCE 416 ---
Photographs show *A. surculosa* to have significant variation in both Guam (www. guamreeflife.com) and American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 417 ---
More research should pursue the potential for species distinction between these two species.

--- SENTENCE 418 ---

--- SENTENCE 432 ---
*Acropora jacquelineae* **Wallace, 1994 (288212)** CoTW CCW

*Acropora jacquelineae* Wallace, 1994 (288212).

--- SENTENCE 433 ---
CoTW CCW Reported – Fenner 2018.

--- SENTENCE 434 ---
Referenced – Kenyon et al. 2011.

--- SENTENCE 435 ---
*Acropora jacquilinae* Wallace, 1994 (288212)

--- SENTENCE 436 ---
Reported – DMWR 2018.

--- SENTENCE 437 ---
**American Sāmoa status** – Present.

--- SENTENCE 438 ---
**Evidence** – Single specimen report (identified by D Fenner).

--- SENTENCE 439 ---
**Distribution** – Taʻū, Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 440 ---
**Nearest confirmed ecoregion** – Sāmoa, Tuvalu, and Tonga.

--- SENTENCE 441 ---
**Vulnerability** – , VU.

--- SENTENCE 442 ---
**Notes** – There have been only two references that report this species, but T Hughes also reports seeing it on Taʻū (T Hughes, pers.

--- SENTENCE 443 ---
An examination of an *A. jacquelineae* sample by D Luck concluded that it was not this species based on the fact that the axials were slightly smaller than reported in Wallace (1999) and that there were many small radial corallites while Wallace (1999) reported there are very few such radials in this species (Luck 2013).

--- SENTENCE 444 ---
Examination of skeletal photographs in Wallace (1999) clearly show that corallites in most of the colony indeed have very few radials, but corallites near the edge of the colony have many radials.

--- SENTENCE 445 ---
The sample analyzed by D Luck was taken from the edge of the colony by D Fenner and D Luck likely did not realize that the sample was taken from the edge.

--- SENTENCE 446 ---
Given that this species is listed as threatened under the ESA, careful attention has been paid to the presence of this species and we believe the evidence in hand is sufficient to conclude its presence in American Sāmoa albeit likely as a rare species.

--- SENTENCE 447 ---

### *Acropora polystoma* **(Brook, 1891) (207050)

*Acropora selago* (Studer, 1879) (207040).

--- SENTENCE 723 ---

--- SENTENCE 724 ---

--- SENTENCE 728 ---

--- SENTENCE 729 ---
**Notes** – Veron (2000) indicates that *A. insignis* is quite different from *A. selago*, which has more appressed radial corallites that are labellate and *A. insignis* has a distinctive coloration.

--- SENTENCE 730 ---
This makes this species easy to distinguish in the water.

--- SENTENCE 731 ---
Given the dispute with this synonym, more work should be conducted to look at the species variation in this group.

--- SENTENCE 732 ---
Randall & Myers (1983) and Randall (1995, 2003) consider *Acropora deliculata* (Brook, 1891)

# *Acropora teres* **(Verrill, 1866) (288255)

--- SENTENCE 769 ---
** CoTW taxon inquirendum

*Acropora teres* (Verrill, 1866) (288255).

--- SENTENCE 770 ---
CoTW Reported – Mayor 1924b; Hoffmeister 1925; USACE 1980; Lamberts 1983; Birkeland 2007a; NMNH 2018.

--- SENTENCE 771 ---
Referenced – Coles et al. 2003.

--- SENTENCE 772 ---
CoTW Reported – USACE 1980.

--- SENTENCE 773 ---
**American Sāmoa status** – Present.

--- SENTENCE 774 ---
**Evidence** – Multiple specimen reports.

--- SENTENCE 775 ---
**Distribution** – American Sāmoa, Ofu, Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 776 ---
**Nearest confirmed ecoregion** – Marshall Islands and Solomon Islands and Bougainville.

--- SENTENCE 777 ---
**Geographical range extension** – Southeast.

--- SENTENCE 778 ---
**Notes** – *Acropora teres* (Verrill, 1886) has not been recorded in American Sāmoa since Hoffmeister (1925) and Lamberts (1983), except for Birkeland (2007a).

--- SENTENCE 779 ---
The observations by Birkeland (2007a) in Ofu Lagoon concerned preliminary identifications without verification making the present record unverifiable.

--- SENTENCE 780 ---
Given that there have been multiple specimen reports documenting this species, it is possible that this species has been extirpated and is no longer present in American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 944 ---

Reported – Birkeland et al. 2003.

--- SENTENCE 946 ---
Reported – Birkeland et al. 2003.

--- SENTENCE 947 ---
*Acropora craterformis* (Gardiner, 1898) (288193)

--- SENTENCE 948 ---
Reported – Birkeland et al. 2003;

--- SENTENCE 949 ---
Coles et al. 2003.

--- SENTENCE 950 ---
*Acropora crateriformis* (Gardiner, 1898)

--- SENTENCE 951 ---
Reported – Hoffmeister 1925; USACE 1980; Lamberts 1983; Birkeland et al. 1987, 2003; Itano and Buckley 1988; Mundy 1996; Craig et al. 2001; Fisk and Birkeland 2002; Coles et al. 2003; DiDonato et al. 2006; Birkeland 2007a; Fenner et al. 2008;

--- SENTENCE 952 ---
Bare et al. 2010; Corals NPAS 2016; BPBM 2018.

--- SENTENCE 953 ---
Referenced – Green et al. 1999; Fisk and Birkeland 2002;

--- SENTENCE 954 ---
Coles et al. 2003; DiDonato et al. 2006; Birkeland 2007a, 2007b; Lovell and McLardy 2008.

--- SENTENCE 955 ---
CoTW CCW Reported – CRED 2011; Fenner and Sudek 2016; DMWR 2018; Fenner 2018; NMNH 2018; QM 2018; Paulay and Brown 2019.

--- SENTENCE 956 ---
Referenced – Kenyon et al. 2011.

--- SENTENCE 957 ---
**American Sāmoa status** – Present.

--- SENTENCE 958 ---
**Evidence** – Multiple specimen reports.

--- SENTENCE 959 ---
**Distribution** – American Sāmoa, Aunuʻu, Manuʻa Islands, Ofu, Ofu/Olosega, Olosega, Taʻū, Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 960 ---
**Nearest confirmed ecoregion** – Sāmoa, Tuvalu, and Tonga.

--- SENTENCE 961 ---
**Vulnerability** – , VU.

--- SENTENCE 962 ---
**Notes** – Wallace (1999) and Veron (2000) note that the only difference between this species and *Isopora cuneata* (Dana, 1846) is colony shape.

--- SENTENCE 963 ---
*Isopora crateriformis* is encrusting while *I. cuneata* is cuneate to branching.

--- SENTENCE 964 ---
Fenner has found only the encrusting/plate shape of *I. crateriformis* in American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 965 ---
*Isopora crateriformis* is abundant to dominant in shallow reef slopes on southwest Tutuila.

**Hoffmeister, 1925 (430668)**

*Montipora vaughani* Hoffmeister, 1925 (430668).

--- SENTENCE 1167 ---
Reported – Hoffmeister 1925; Lamberts 1983; Fenner 2018; NMNH 2018.

--- SENTENCE 1168 ---
**American Sāmoa status** – Present.

--- SENTENCE 1169 ---
**Evidence** – Type specimen location.

--- SENTENCE 1170 ---
**Distribution** – American Sāmoa, Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 1171 ---
**Nearest confirmed ecoregion** – Not available.

--- SENTENCE 1172 ---
**Notes** – This species is easily identified but has one difference with *M. foveolata*, i.e., rows of corallites that are closer together within rows and farther apart between rows.

--- SENTENCE 1173 ---
However, some colonies have areas like this and other areas like *M. foveolata*.

--- SENTENCE 1174 ---
Veron et al. (2019) believe this species is probable synonym of *M. foveolata*.

--- SENTENCE 2879 ---

--- SENTENCE 2880 ---

--- SENTENCE 2881 ---
The type specimen of *P. lutea* has not been examined, and E Turak (pers.

--- SENTENCE 2882 ---
comm.) indicates that the label on the presumed type in the Paris Natural History Museum may have been moved from a different skeleton.

--- SENTENCE 2883 ---
Thus, there are significant taxonomic challenges for this species and more work needed to sort the differences.

### *Porites monticulosa* **Dana, 1846 (367816)** CoTW

*Porites monticulosa* Dana, 1846 (367816).

--- SENTENCE 2884 ---
CoTW Reported – Birkeland et al. 1987; CRED 2011;

--- SENTENCE 2885 ---
Corals NPAS 2016; DMWR 2018; Fenner 2018.

--- SENTENCE 2886 ---
Referenced – Coles et al. 2003; DiDonato et al. 2006; Birkeland 2007b; Lovell and McLardy 2008.

--- SENTENCE 2887 ---
**American Sāmoa status** – Present.

--- SENTENCE 2888 ---
**Evidence** – Single specimen report (identified by D Fenner).

--- SENTENCE 2889 ---
**Distribution** – American Sāmoa, Swains, Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 2890 ---
**Nearest confirmed ecoregion** – Sāmoa, Tuvalu, and Tonga.

--- SENTENCE 2891 ---
**Notes** – This species is very similar to *Porites rus* (Forskål, 1775).

--- SENTENCE 2892 ---
The type specimen of *P. monticulosa* in the Yale Peabody Museum is a short, thick, rounded column.

--- SENTENCE 2893 ---
It can be reliably separated from *P. rus* in many locations in the Pacific, including American Sāmoa and Hawaii (Fenner 2005).

--- SENTENCE 2894 ---
However, there are no reliable microscopic features that distinguish it from *P. rus* and the species concept sensu Veron (2000) and Veron et al. (2019).

*Millepora exaesa* **Forsskål, 1775 (210728)**

*Millepora exaesa* Forsskål, 1775 (210728).

--- SENTENCE 3153 ---
Reported – Green et al. 1997; Fisk and Birkeland 2002; DiDonato et al. 2006; Birkeland 2007a; Fenner et al. 2008; Cor-

als NPAS 2016; Fenner 2018.

--- SENTENCE 3154 ---

--- SENTENCE 3155 ---
Reported – DMWR 2018.

--- SENTENCE 3156 ---
*Millepora tuberosa* Boschma, 1966 (210732) heterotypic synonym.

--- SENTENCE 3157 ---
Reported – Birkeland et al. 1987, 2003;

--- SENTENCE 3158 ---
Birkeland and Belliveau 2000; Coles et al. 2003; DMWR 2018; Fenner 2018.

--- SENTENCE 3159 ---
Referenced – Green et al. 1999; Coles et al. 2003; Birkeland 2007b; Lovell and McLardy 2008; Kenyon et al. 2011.

--- SENTENCE 3160 ---
**American Sāmoa status** – Present.

--- SENTENCE 3161 ---
**Evidence** – Single specimen report (identified by D Fenner).

--- SENTENCE 3162 ---
**Distribution** – American Sāmoa, Aunuʻu, Manuʻa Islands, Ofu, Ofu/ Olosega, Olosega, Taʻū, Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 3163 ---
**Nearest confirmed ecoregion** – Not available.

--- SENTENCE 3164 ---
**Notes** – *Millepora exaesa* is yellow-brown with occasional light green or pink, encrusts rubble, can have larger bumps, and found in lagoons.

--- SENTENCE 3165 ---
The synonym *Millepora tuberosa* Boschma, 1966 is purple encrusting sheets on hard substrate that can grow quite large and is found on slopes.

--- SENTENCE 3166 ---
Skeletons can easily be confused, but live colonies are distinguishable.

--- SENTENCE 3167 ---
For taxonomic details, see Randall and Cheng (1984).

--- SENTENCE 3168 ---
We believe that the taxonomy of this group needs to be revised and that the synonym *M. tuberosa* Boschma, 1966 may deserve to be resurrected for specimens outside the Red Sea (Arrigoni et al. 2018b).

--- SENTENCE 3169 ---
The photographed specimen of this species reported in Corals NPAS (2016) appears to have an incorrect identification and should be *Millepora platyphylla* Hemprich & Ehrenberg, 1834.

--- SENTENCE 3170 ---
# *Millepora intricata* **Milne Edwards, 1860 (210727)**

*Millepora intricata* Milne Edwards, 1860 (210727).

--- SENTENCE 3171 ---
Referenced – Lovell and McLardy 2008.

--- SENTENCE 3172 ---
*Millepora murrayi* Quelch, 1884 (292201) possible heterotypic synonym.

--- SENTENCE 3173 ---
Reported – DiDonato et al. 2006;

--- SENTENCE 3174 ---
Corals NPAS 2016; DMWR 2018; Fenner 2018.

--- SENTENCE 3175 ---
Referenced – Lovell and McLardy 2008.

--- SENTENCE 3176 ---
**American Sāmoa status** – Present.

--- SENTENCE 3177 ---
**Evidence** – Single specimen report (identified by D Fenner).

--- SENTENCE 3178 ---
**Distribution** – American Sāmoa, Ofu, Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 3179 ---
**Nearest confirmed ecoregion** – Not available.

--- SENTENCE 3180 ---
**Notes** – Colonies of *M. intricata* have less obvious ogives while the synonym *Millepora murrayi* Quelch, 1884 has very obvious ogives, which are downward curving branches with upward growing branches on the upper edge.

--- SENTENCE 3181 ---
Fenner has seen *M. intricata* in the Philippines which could be mistaken for *M. murrayi*.

--- SENTENCE 3182 ---
Razak and Hoeksema (2003) were correct that the colonies reported from Indonesia were *M. intricata*, but it is possible that *M. murrayi* is also present and valid.

--- SENTENCE 3183 ---
We believe this group needs to be revisited and the synonym *M. murrayi* may deserve to be resurrected.

--- SENTENCE 3184 ---
For more taxonomic details, see Randall and Cheng (1984).

--- SENTENCE 3185 ---
**Hemprich & Ehrenberg, 1834 (210730)

--- SENTENCE 3186 ---
**

*Millepora platyphylla* cf.

--- SENTENCE 3187 ---
Hemprich & Ehrenberg, 1834 (210730).

--- SENTENCE 3218 ---

--- SENTENCE 3219 ---

--- SENTENCE 3220 ---
Based on the limited observations and the lack of a confirmed identification in the BPBM collection, we consider this species as possibly present in American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 3225 ---

--- SENTENCE 3228 ---
**Nearest confirmed ecoregion** – Sāmoa, Tuvalu, and Tonga.

--- SENTENCE 3232 ---

--- SENTENCE 3233 ---
Based on these limited observations and its close similarity to another species, we determined this species as possibly present in American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 3307 ---
Birkeland et al. 1987; Maragos et al. 1994; Kenyon et al. 2010; Corals NPAS 2016.

--- SENTENCE 3308 ---
Referenced – Coles et al. 2003; DiDonato et al. 2006; Birkeland 2007b.

--- SENTENCE 3309 ---
CoTW CCW Reported – Birkeland et al. 1987.

--- SENTENCE 3310 ---
**American Sāmoa status** – Possibly present.

--- SENTENCE 3311 ---
**Evidence** – Single specimen report (identified by A Lamberts).

--- SENTENCE 3312 ---
**Distribution** – American Sāmoa, Aunuʻu, Rose Atoll, Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 3313 ---
**Nearest confirmed ecoregion** – Madagascar north.

--- SENTENCE 3314 ---
**Geographical range extension** – East although Veron et al. (2019) stated this species distribution was uncertain due to taxonomic uncertainties, significant geographical range extension.

--- SENTENCE 3315 ---
**Notes** – *Acropora squarrosa* is a Red Sea and western Indian Ocean species, and both Wallace (1999) and Veron (2000) report that it is endemic to the Red Sea.

--- SENTENCE 3316 ---
Based on a sample identified by A Lamberts and in situ reports (Birkeland et al. 1987; Maragos et al. 1994), we believe that this species is possibly present.

--- SENTENCE 3317 ---
However, caution may be warranted on this species due to the later work not available to Lamberts (1983), Birkeland et al. 1987, and Maragos et al. (1994).

# *Pavona diffluens* **(Lamarck, 1816) (207295)** CoTW

*Pavona diffluens* (Lamarck, 1816) (207295).

--- SENTENCE 3600 ---
CoTW Reported – Corals NPAS 2016; DMWR 2018; Fenner 2018.

--- SENTENCE 3601 ---
Referenced – DiDonato et al. 2006; Birkeland 2007b; Lovell and McLardy 2008; Kenyon et al. 2011.

--- SENTENCE 3602 ---
CoTW Reported – Birkeland et al. 1987; Montgomery et al. 2019.

--- SENTENCE 3603 ---
Referenced – Coles et al. 2003.

--- SENTENCE 3604 ---
**American Sāmoa status** – Possibly present.

--- SENTENCE 3605 ---
**Evidence** – Single specimen report (identified by D Fenner).

--- SENTENCE 3606 ---
**Distribution** – American Sāmoa, Ofu/Olosega, Olosega, Taʻū, Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 3607 ---
**Nearest confirmed ecoregion** – Socotra Archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 3608 ---
**Geographical range extension** – Southeast, significant geographical range extension.

--- SENTENCE 3609 ---
**Vulnerability** – , VU.

--- SENTENCE 3610 ---
**Mesophotic record** – 44 m depth (Montgomery et al. 2019).

--- SENTENCE 3611 ---
**Notes** – This species is similar to *Pavona explanulata*

--- SENTENCE 3612 ---
(Lamarck, 1816) and *Pavona gigantea* Verrill, 1869.

--- SENTENCE 3613 ---
The type locality of this species is the Red Sea, from where it is well known.

--- SENTENCE 3614 ---
Randall (1995, 2003) has also reported this species from Guam and the Marianas.

--- SENTENCE 3615 ---
Veron (2014) reports that colonies in the Pacific Ocean are likely belonging to an undescribed species that is similar to *P. diffluens*, but with different corallite sizes.

--- SENTENCE 3616 ---
comm.) reports that there appear to be no differences in corallite sizes or features between the specimen in the DMWR collection and Red Sea skeleton shown by Veron (2000, vol 2: 188) and Sheppard and Sheppard (1991: 88).

--- SENTENCE 3617 ---
Photos of the live colonies appear to be within the range of variation of Red Sea colonies (sensu Veron 2000).

--- SENTENCE 3618 ---
Fenner (2015) also reported this species with a level of species ID uncertainty.

--- SENTENCE 3619 ---
NOAA currently implements the species concept as described in Veron (2014) thereby not affording the protection of the ESA to corals similar to *P. diffluens* in the Pacific (NOAA 2014).

--- SENTENCE 3620 ---
Given the protection status of *P. diffluens* under the ESA, more analysis is warranted on its type and samples from American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 3621 ---
Here, we apply the precautionary principle to this species as possibly present in American Sāmoa until Pacific specimens are confirmed to belong to *P. diffluens* or can be described as a new species.

--- SENTENCE 3622 ---

#### **Family Fungiidae Dana, 1846 Genus** *Cycloseris* **Milne Edwards & Haime, 1849**

*Cycloseris explanulata* **(van der Horst, 1922) (716292)**

*Psammocora explanulata* van der Horst, 1922 (207265) homotypic synonym.

--- SENTENCE 3656 ---
CoTW Reported – Coles et al. 2003; Corals NPAS 2016.

--- SENTENCE 3657 ---
**American Sāmoa status** – Possibly present.

--- SENTENCE 3658 ---
**Evidence** – Single report.

--- SENTENCE 3659 ---

--- SENTENCE 3660 ---

--- SENTENCE 3661 ---
**Geographical range extension** – Between two disjunct ecoregions.

--- SENTENCE 3662 ---
**Notes** – There remains some significant taxonomic disagreement with the placement of *Psammocora explanulata* van der Horst, 1922 in the genus *Cycloseris*.

--- SENTENCE 3663 ---
Benzoni et al. (2012a) moved this species into *Cycloseris* based on genetic evidence, but Veron et al. (2019) state that this species does not have the genus-level characters of *Cycloseris* and therefore retains this species in *Psammocora*.

--- SENTENCE 3664 ---
However, original genus descriptions are often simple and based on the characters of the type species.

--- SENTENCE 3665 ---
They may need to be revised based on new information and the addition of other species.

--- SENTENCE 3666 ---
A taxonomic revision seems warranted in this circumstance (Benzoni et al. 2012).

--- SENTENCE 3667 ---
The identification of this species can be similar to *Cycloseris wellsi* (Veron & Pichon, 1980) (see the note for that species for further information), and Benzoni et al.

--- SENTENCE 3668 ---
(2012) report it may be confused with *Cycloseris mokai* (Hoeksema, 1989)

--- SENTENCE 3669 ---
although field identifications are distinct.

--- SENTENCE 3670 ---

### **Family Psammocoridae Chevalier & Beauvais, 1987 Genus** *Psammocora* **Dana, 1846**

*Psammocora haimiana*

--- SENTENCE 4061 ---
**Milne Edwards & Haime, 1851 (718603)

--- SENTENCE 4062 ---
** CoTW

*Psammacora folium* Umbgrove, 1939 (207259)

--- SENTENCE 4063 ---
Reported – USACE 1980.

--- SENTENCE 4064 ---
*Psammocora folium* Umbgrove, 1939 (207259) heterotypic synonym.

--- SENTENCE 4065 ---
Reported – Lamberts 1983.

--- SENTENCE 4066 ---
*Psammocora haimeana* Milne Edwards & Haime, 1851 (207262) wrong species spelling.

--- SENTENCE 4067 ---
Reported – Birkeland et al. 1987, 2003; Maragos et al. 1994; Mundy 1996; Fisk and Birkeland 2002;

--- SENTENCE 4068 ---
Coles et al. 2003; Birkeland 2007a; Fenner et al. 2008; Kenyon et al. 2010; CRED 2011; Corals NPAS 2016; DMWR 2018; NMNH 2018.

--- SENTENCE 4069 ---
Referenced – Green et al. 1999; Fisk and Birkeland 2002;

--- SENTENCE 4070 ---
Coles et al. 2003; DiDonato et al. 2006; Birkeland 2007a, 2007b; Lovell and McLardy 2008.

--- SENTENCE 4071 ---
*Psammocora hameana* Milne Edwards & Haime, 1851 (207262)

--- SENTENCE 4072 ---
[sic] wrong species spelling.

--- SENTENCE 4073 ---
Reported – Fenner et al. 2008.

--- SENTENCE 4074 ---
**American Sāmoa status** – Possibly present.

--- SENTENCE 4075 ---
**Evidence** – Single specimen report (identified by A Lamberts).

--- SENTENCE 4076 ---
**Distribution** – American Sāmoa, Aunuʻu, Manuʻa Islands, Ofu, Ofu/Olosega, Olosega, Rose Atoll, Swains, Taʻū, Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 4077 ---
**Nearest confirmed ecoregion** – Vanuatu and Tuamotu Archipelago west, central Pacific.

--- SENTENCE 4078 ---
**Geographical range extension** – Between two disjunct ecoregions although Veron et al. (2019) strongly predicted the presence of this species in the Sāmoa, Tuvalu, and Tonga ecoregion.

--- SENTENCE 4079 ---
**Notes** – Benzoni et al. (2010) discuss at length several species of *Psammocora*.

--- SENTENCE 4080 ---
They reported that the species name *P. haimeana* sensu Klunzinger 1879 seems to be an incorrect spelling of *P. haimiana*, but that this misspelling has been propagated in the literature since Veron and Pichon (1976).

--- SENTENCE 4081 ---
However, the name *P. haimeana* is not as simple as an incorrect spelling as they document through morphological and molecular analyses that specimens identified as *P. haimeana* are actually *Psammocora profundacella* Gardiner, 1898.

--- SENTENCE 4082 ---
This indicates that all the colonies listed here as *P. haimeana* are likely *P. profundacella* and should not be considered as evidence for the presence of *P. haimiana*.

--- SENTENCE 4083 ---
However, A Lamberts identified a specimen as *P. folium* which appears to be a synonym of *P. haimiana*, which Benzoni et al. (2010) confirm as correct.

--- SENTENCE 4084 ---
Despite this sample, we believe that the uncertainty of the identity of this species leaves uncertainty of its presence in American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 4085 ---
We should further note that the name *P. haimiana* has not been directly reported in American Sāmoa, which historically has been used incorrectly for the species *P. digitata* (see note under *P. digitata*).

--- SENTENCE 4086 ---

Veron et al. (2019) reported that this species may be difficult to distinguish from *Lobophyllia ishigakiensis* (Veron, 1990) and *Sclerophyllia maxima* (Sheppard & Salm, 1988), the latter not reported in American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 4177 ---
It is possible that this report was a misidentified colony of *L. ishigakiensis*.

--- SENTENCE 4178 ---
Veron et al. (2019) maintain *A. hillae* as valid and recognizes *Acanthastrea bowerbanki* Milne Edwards & Haime, 1857 as a separate and valid species.

--- SENTENCE 4179 ---
Arrigoni et al. (2016) synonymized both species under *H. bowerbanki*.

#### **Discussion**

There can be considerable uncertainty in identifying corals due to several underlying problems (Bernard 1902; Veron 1993, 1995, 2015; Forsman et al. 2015).

--- SENTENCE 4560 ---
Some species are inherently difficult to identify or to discern from congeners based on minor morphological differences or plastic characters making taxonomic differences difficult to discern (Todd et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 4561 ---
Additionally, people may have considerable variation in identification skills and taxonomic knowledge leading to incorrect identifications.

--- SENTENCE 4562 ---
Finally, names of species or a species concept can change over time, particularly in groups that have a historical nomenclatural confusion such as *Psammocora haimeana* as discussed below.

--- SENTENCE 4563 ---
This makes it difficult to judge if species identifications are correct without documentation of the observation.

--- SENTENCE 4564 ---
For specimens observed in situ, there is no way to truly verify the observation as correct, so we can only rely on the expertise of the observer, the frequency of the species observed, and a subjective likelihood of the species observation.

--- SENTENCE 4565 ---
For reports that have photographic documentation, the species observation may be verifiable, but photographic documentation is often inconclusive as the appropriate species level characters are not always visible.

--- SENTENCE 4566 ---
Reports that rely on the identification of a collected specimen can be the most powerful for conclusive documentation.

--- SENTENCE 4567 ---
However, even with collections, caution should still be exercised because individuals vary in their ability and experience with coral taxonomy and identification.

--- SENTENCE 4568 ---
The most conclusive documentation of a species presence is the collection of a specimen that becomes the type specimen of that species.

--- SENTENCE 4569 ---
This also includes species and its type that are synonymized with another species.

--- SENTENCE 4570 ---
Type specimens described from American Sāmoa include *Acropora tutuilensis* (synonym *Acropora abrotanoides*), *Acropora pagoensis* (synonym *Acropora eurystoma* ), *Alveopora allingi*, *Astreopora cucullata*, *Montipora berryi*, *Montipora vaughani*, *Pocillopora setchelli* (synonym *Pocillopora brevicornis*), *Porites horizontalata*, and *Porites randalli*.

--- SENTENCE 4571 ---
The spelling of species names and the interpretation of the exact spelling or the assumed name can also be important in determining the likelihood of the presence of that given species.

--- SENTENCE 4572 ---
In the course of this analysis, two examples demonstrate the need for keeping track of the spellings and how to determine the meaning of the intended species.

--- SENTENCE 4573 ---
One example is the reported species name *Acropora caniculata*

--- SENTENCE 4574 ---
(DiDonato et al. 2006; Corals NPAS 2016) which is not a valid species name.

--- SENTENCE 4575 ---
The fuzzy match algorithm used in the WoRMS REST webservice suggested that the best match is *Acropora paniculata* which is reasonable if one assumes this is a simple typo.

--- SENTENCE 4576 ---
However, Corals NPAS (2016) provides photographic evidence of this species and the photo is clearly not *A. paniculata*.

--- SENTENCE 4577 ---
Instead, the specimen in the photo more closely resembles *Acropora nasuta*, which has a synonym of *A. canaliculata* that may serve as the likely misspelled name.

--- SENTENCE 4578 ---
This highlights the need to carefully review all fuzzy name matches, particularly when a species is reported with no verifiable evidence.

--- SENTENCE 4579 ---
Another example is the reported name *Psammocora haimeana* which had been a historical name used and reported.

--- SENTENCE 4580 ---
However, we now know that *P. haimeana* has been a misspelled name of *P. haimiana* starting with Klunzinger (1879) and perpetuated since.

--- SENTENCE 4581 ---
Further evidence now shows that colonies reported as *P. haimeana* are actually *Psammocora profundacella* (Benzoni et al. 2010).

--- SENTENCE 4582 ---
This shows that published names, especially those that do not have type specimens, further complicating proper identification, should be used carefully.

--- SENTENCE 4583 ---
Coral taxonomy and identification are fraught with complex difficulties and highly variable, and sometimes poorly documented characters.

--- SENTENCE 4584 ---
Taxonomy is largely based on the morphology of type specimens; therefore, additional comparison between a specimen and the species type description is usually needed to confirm an identification.

--- SENTENCE 4585 ---
An example of this situation is *Acropora humilis* and *Acropora globiceps* where we believe all previous reports of *A. humilis* in American Sāmoa are actually *A. globiceps* based on evaluation of the type specimens of both species by D Fenner.

--- SENTENCE 4586 ---
These comparisons are rarely done due to researchers being unaware of how to find the type specimens or to the inability to access type specimens, if they are even available at all.

--- SENTENCE 4587 ---
Some coral type specimens are of poor quality and increase the likelihood of different interpretations (Veron et al. 2019).

--- SENTENCE 4588 ---
Further complicating coral taxonomy and identification is the uncertain evolutionary history of this group, and the general lack of concordance between morphological and molecular systematics of scleractinians (Fukami et al. 2004).

--- SENTENCE 4589 ---
Coral taxonomy is based on morphological characters of the skeleton; however, these characters do not always delineate families or species well.

--- SENTENCE 4590 ---
For example, early application of molecular phylogenetic analyses to scleractinian corals revealed two major groups, complex and robust, based on DNA that did not correspond to morphologically-based suborders (Romano and Palumbi 1996; Romano and Cairns 2000).

--- SENTENCE 4591 ---
Subsequent studies found that most families of corals based on morphological characters were not monophyletic based on genetic data (Fukami et al. 2008).

--- SENTENCE 4592 ---
Adding to the confusion, there is concordance between molecular data and morphological characters in some groups (Wallace et al. 2007; Flot et al. 2008; Forsman et al. 2010; Benzoni et al. 2012b; Marti-Puig et al. 2014; Forsman et al. 2015), but not others and even variable conclusions among studies using the same groups of species (Miller and Benzie 1997; Forsman et al. 2009, 2017; Pinzón et al. 2013; Combosch and Vollmer 2015; Johnston et al. 2017).

--- SENTENCE 4593 ---
Veron (1995) proposed this inconsistency originates from corals having undergone reticulate evolution where species populations hybridized during periods of overlap separated by periods of isolation confounding species boundaries.

--- SENTENCE 4594 ---
A range of contradictory studies argue in support of or against reticulate evolution in corals (Johnston et al. 2017a; van Oppen et al. 2001; Vollmer and Palumbi 2002; Flot et al. 2011; Combosch and Vollmer 2015).

--- SENTENCE 4595 ---
Ultimately, whatever the cause, all morphological traits are based on the underlying genetic code with environmental influences, so morphological and molecular characters have to agree at some level, and there is a need to combine both approaches to further our understanding of species boundaries and resolve the ongoing "species problem" (Bernard 1902) in scleractinian corals (Fukami et al. 2004, 2008;

--- SENTENCE 4596 ---
Forsman et al. 2010; Stat et al. 2012; Kitahara et al. 2016; Johnston et al. 2017).

--- SENTENCE 4597 ---
Studies that show a discordance between morphological and molecular approaches should be viewed with caution due to the genes examined, false assumptions of the mechanism of evolution, and/or the plasticity and appropriateness of both the morphological and molecular characters examined (Losos et al. 2012).

--- SENTENCE 4598 ---
We believe this annotated checklist provides a foundation for further morphological and genetic analysis of the corals present within American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 4599 ---
Despite the difficulties of identification, this checklist is our best estimate of the species we believe are present in American Sāmoa given the caveat of different levels of identification uncertainty.

--- SENTENCE 4600 ---
These results provide a comprehensive list of species in an orderly fashion that can be further analyzed and/or reinterpreted by others interested in coral species distribution.

--- SENTENCE 4601 ---
We report there has been 745 unique names and spellings of species used for American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 4602 ---
Of these, 538 represent valid species names (including synonyms), of which 377 are currently accepted names.

--- SENTENCE 4603 ---
Among these 377 species, we conclude that there are 251 species present and 91 species possibly present.

--- SENTENCE 4604 ---
In addition, there are 20 species of uncertain presence, nine species likely not present, and six species considered incorrectly reported and not present.

--- SENTENCE 4605 ---
A significant factor in determining the number of species in any location is a consistent use of accepted taxonomy.

--- SENTENCE 4606 ---
If we consider differences in the taxonomy, the number of species present in American Sāmoa can change.

--- SENTENCE 4607 ---
The main differences between differing taxonomies include two distinct types of synonyms.

--- SENTENCE 4608 ---
One includes homotypic synonyms where the species identification is not in dispute, but rather the placement of that species within a certain genus, thereby creating a dispute in species name, but no dispute in that species being a discrete taxonomic entity.

--- SENTENCE 4609 ---
Of these species presented here, there are 54 homotypic species with CoTW disagreeing on 40 species.

--- SENTENCE 4610 ---
Thirty-four include species where CoTW does not acknowledge the movement of a species to a different genus and six species where CoTW moves species to a different genus but is not currently recognized by WLS.

--- SENTENCE 4611 ---
The other type of synonym difference includes heterotypic synonyms where two names are based on two type specimens that have been combined into a single species.

--- SENTENCE 4612 ---
In this circumstance, different experts based on their experience and knowledge of that species may have differing opinions on the validity of the synonyms.

--- SENTENCE 4613 ---
Of these species presented here, there are 72 heterotypic species with CoTW disagreeing on 20 species split in WLS and eight species that CoTW splits but are not in WLS.

--- SENTENCE 4614 ---
A fundamental difference is that taxonomic changes in WLS are based on references in peer-reviewed journals and those in CoTW are not.

--- SENTENCE 4615 ---
This is important since taxonomic changes based on the ICZN need to be published in printed media such as journals and books or they need to have a Zoobank registration, while those in electronic media only without a Zoobank registration are not valid.

--- SENTENCE 4616 ---
While the on-line version of CoTW (Veron et al. 2019) is not currently compliant with ICZN, it does provide insight to different expert opinion on species concepts.

--- SENTENCE 4617 ---
Here we report new records for American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 4618 ---
*Montipora marshallensis* Wells, 1954 is reported as a probable synonym of *Montipora crassituberculata* Bernard, 1897 by CoTW and is included in the eight heterotypic synonyms discussed previously, but this synonymization creates the first time this name to be used in relation to American Sāmoa and is not included in the totals listed in this study.

--- SENTENCE 4619 ---
Additionally, we report four new records documented by D Fenner not previously reported in American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 4620 ---
These records include *Acanthastrea subechinata* Veron, 2000 (Figure 2a), *Favites paraflexuosus* Veron, 2000 (Figure 2b), *Echinophyllia echinoporoides* Veron & Pichon, 1980 (Figure 2c), and *Turbinaria irregularis* Bernard, 1896 (Figure 2d).

--- SENTENCE 4621 ---
The evidence presented here is sufficient to conclude these species are present.

--- SENTENCE 4622 ---
We report a total of 342 species present or possibly present for American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 4623 ---
If one were to accept the species with taxonomic differences of opinion from Veron et al. (2019), the species number decreases by approximately 12 species.

--- SENTENCE 4624 ---
Further, Veron et al. (2019) included only zooxanthellate scleractinian corals thereby reducing the number by another eight species (four azooxanhellate dendrophyllids and four milleporids).

--- SENTENCE 4625 ---
This allows comparable numbers of 322 species to the 313 species reported by Veron et al. (2019) from the Sāmoa, Tuvalu,

--- SENTENCE 4626 ---
Presumably, any in-depth analysis for the other islands within this ecoregion will report other species not found in American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 4627 ---
This would indicate that the species richness reported in Veron et al. (2019) is likely an underestimate for this ecoregion.

--- SENTENCE 4628 ---
It is difficult to determine the amount of this underestimate, but may indicate that the species richness is closer to that found in

!

--- SENTENCE 4629 ---
New scleractinian records for American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 4630 ---
**a** *Acanthastrea subechinata* Veron, 2000 **b** *Favites paraflexuosus* Veron, 2000 **c** *Echinophyllia echinoporoides* Veron & Pichon, 1980 **d** *Turbinaria irregularis* Bernard, 1896.

--- SENTENCE 4631 ---
Photographs by D Fenner.

--- SENTENCE 4632 ---
Micronesia, the Coral Sea, or Vanuatu (Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 4633 ---
This also indicates that the species richness is still much lower than the Coral Triangle and the broader geographical pattern of species richness across the Pacific remains (Veron et al. 2015).

--- SENTENCE 4634 ---
The known diversity of an area is largely a product of effort.

--- SENTENCE 4635 ---
With increasing effort, more species are documented up to an asymptote (Colwell et al. 2012).

--- SENTENCE 4636 ---
Both the present list and the estimates in Veron et al. (2019) are likely to be underestimates of the true diversity within any single region.

--- SENTENCE 4637 ---
Using the ecoregions from Veron et al. (2019), we show geographical range extension records for 66 species considered present or possibly present.

--- SENTENCE 4638 ---
The direction of these range extensions are 61% to the east, while 21% close the gap between two disjunct ecoregions.

--- SENTENCE 4639 ---
Fewer species are extended south (3%), southeast (11%), and southwest (4%).

--- SENTENCE 4640 ---
There are no species that are only extended to the west.

--- SENTENCE 4641 ---
Of these range extensions, three species were considerable.

--- SENTENCE 4642 ---
The nearest confirmed ecoregion for *Acropora squarrosa* was Madagascar north, while *Pavona gigantea* was Marshall Islands and Galapagos Islands and *Pavona diffluens* was Socotra Archipelago in the Indian Ocean.

--- SENTENCE 4643 ---
While shallow coral reef corals are relatively well described, mesophotic corals are poorly described in American Sāmoa.

--- SENTENCE 4644 ---
The maximum reported depth of a zooxanthellate coral is 165 m at Johnston Atoll (Kahng and Maragos 2006), and 19 of 66 coral spe-

--- SENTENCE 4655 ---
The number of corals assessed for extinction risk.

--- SENTENCE 4656 ---
The number of corals and the percentages (Unknown (Unk) = DD + NE; Threatened (Thr) = VU + EN + CE).

cies were reported from mesophotic depths in the Hawaiian Archipelgo (Spalding et al. 2019).

--- SENTENCE 4657 ---
Of the 51 reviewed studies that report corals from American Sāmoa, only four have reported corals from mesophotic depths (Hoffmeister 1925; Lamberts 1983;

--- SENTENCE 4658 ---
Bare et al. 2010; Montgomery et al. 2019).

--- SENTENCE 4659 ---
These limited results report 90 mesophotic species to a maximum depth of 53 m, leaving a significant portion of MCE depths unexplored and hence a large information gap in the coral species diversity.

--- SENTENCE 4660 ---
Bare et al. (2010) reported additional species and colonies deeper than previously reported, but the species are tentative identifications from low-resolution video so are not included here.

--- SENTENCE 4661 ---
Most of the mesophotic corals reported are considered depth generalist species with almost all of them reported from shallow reefs.

--- SENTENCE 4662 ---
Given the maximum depth of these species reports, it is very likely that the number of species recorded from American Sāmoa will increase with more surveys on MCEs, particularly from the lower mesophotic depth range.

--- SENTENCE 4663 ---
Corals have long been threatened from many sources of anthropogenic factors including overfishing, land-based sources of pollution, development, and climate change (Pandolfi 2003; Bellwood et al. 2004; Halpern et al. 2007; Brainard et al. 2013).

--- SENTENCE 4664 ---
Based on these threats, 845 scleractinian, helioporid, tubiporid, and milleporid coral species were assessed for extinction risk using the IUCN Red List Categories and Criteria.

--- SENTENCE 4665 ---
Carpenter et al. (2008) estimated that 27% of the global species of corals were threatened (Table 2).

--- SENTENCE 4666 ---
Of the species considered present or possibly present in American Sāmoa, we estimate that 17.5% of the species are considered threatened while only 14% of the known mesophotic corals are categorized as threatened.

--- SENTENCE 4667 ---
This seems in line with the determination that American Sāmoaʻs coral reefs are in "good" condition (NOAA and UM-CEP 2018).

--- SENTENCE 4668 ---
Overall, this seems to suggest the corals in American Sāmoa may be doing better than corals on a global scale; however, it should be noted that there is a higher percentage of corals that have an unknown assessment.

--- SENTENCE 4669 ---
The vertical distribution of species may play an important role in their potential risk of extinction, particularly for species that are considered depth generalist in which most of the mesophotic corals reported in American Sāmoa are considered.

--- SENTENCE 4704 ---
These reports provide resource managers additional information to further evaluate species distributions.
